        high population densities, with double or triple rows and narrow in-row spacings.



                                                    66
In order to get homogenous canopy cover, it is very important to do the basics right, such as
having level fields; well drained soils; no compaction; the correct amounts of inputs; and sowing to
high standards. However, it is just as important to maintain good canopy cover in your fields
through gapping, thinning, top dressing, weed control and good pest management.

   9.6. Permanence
It is vitally important to go through the process described in Biblical Key 6 “Staking your Claim”,
which is about taking spiritual authority over the land God has given to you, through prayer and
dedication. However, it is just as important to go through the process of staking your claim
physically to establish permanence in your fields.
         Isaiah 28:10 “For He says, ‘Order on order, order on order, line on line, line on line, a little
         here, a little there.’”
         Proverbs 4:11,12 “I will instruct you in the way of wisdom and lead you along straight paths.
         When you walk, you won’t be held back; when you run, you won’t stumble.”
Permanence brings order into our fields and allows us to build effectively and efficiently, line upon
line. Permanence is established by using both measuring ropes and permanent pegs, ideally at
75cm intervals. Permanence ensures that planting is done accurately on the same planting line or
band every season. In fields where we would typically grow field crops like maize, sorghum,
soyabeans, groundnuts etc, we encourage farmers to place permanent pegs preferably after every
10 rows of 75cm. Vegetables on the other hand are much more intensively farmed, with frequent
rotations and rotations within rotations, and so permanence is established on every 75cm row line.
The benefits of permanence include:
        1) improved soil fertility in the planting area
        2) leftover nutrients are available to the following crops
        3) improved soil structure in the planting area
        4) decomposing root channels allow for greatly improved aeration and permeability
        5) soil pH is improving all the time in the planting area
        6) compaction only ever takes place in the walkways
        7) soil disturbance is minimal resulting in greatly reduced weed incidence etc
Ultimately, permanence will improve your crops and your profitability. The importance of
permanence cannot be stressed enough, and yet this is one key part of Farming God's Way
technology that seems to be poorly implemented. Imagine building a house without a
foundation…you would never do that!!! The same is true with farming. We need a good solid
permanent foundation to be able to work from, so that we can build into a prosperous future.
Wherever Farming God's Way is implemented wholeheartedly, we see incredible results and this is
one of those high standards principles, which is an essential part of the equation. Straight lines in
Farming God's Way fields look fantastic, but they are not like that for looks. Instead they are in
straight lines because we want to achieve permanence. This is very important to understand and
implement right from the very start and then you will have permanence forever.
Wherever a garden is planted… “nyoka nyoka”… or in “crooked ways”, that is not done “to high
standards”, and you don’t get any of the benefits. If your curved row causes permanence to be
missed by as little as 10cm, the plants won’t be able to access the previous season’s leftover
nutrients or improved soil pH or fertility or organic matter and this will affect your ability to have
sustainable profitability. So be diligent and follow the step by step procedure carefully from the
beginning.
God’s Ways are the ancient paths that God has established since the very beginning.
         Proverbs 3:5-6 “Trust in the Lord with all your heart and do not lean on your own
         understanding. In all your ways acknowledge Him, and He will make your paths straight.”
         Do you trust Him enough to change your ways? Do you have the humility to lean on His
         understanding? Are you willing to acknowledge and follow Him in all of your ways? If so, He
         will make your paths straight.

10. Implementing Farming God's Way - Step by Step Procedure

    10.1.       Small Scale Farmer Kit
Tools
    2 to 4 Hoes – sharpen the blades and repair handles
    60cm stick for making the measuring rope
    Measuring rope with 60cm markers preferably as long as your field – maximum 50m long
    Downslope rope to maintain straight edge or preferably a measuring rope with 75cm markers
    Large sheet of paper or book to get a right angle
    2 × 75cm sticks
    30 × Permanent pegs 20cm long to be placed after every 10 rows.
    2 × 500ml tins, cups or bowls – advised for most soils in poor condition where no top dressing
     is done. Otherwise use 2 × 350ml containers
    2 Teaspoons or 5ml cup
    2 Tablespoons or 15ml cup

Inputs required
Seed - 25kg/ha (10kg/acre) for maize, for others see alternative crops for guidelines
a) Organic fertiliser – no top dressing
    Woodash – 5 bags/ha
    Manure - aiming for 6-8 t/ha yield put on 500ml × 22,222 holes = 222 × 50kg bags
   Or Compost - aiming for 7-9 t/ha yield put on 500ml × 22,222 holes = 222 × 50kg bags
   Or Anthill Soil - aiming for 1-3 t/ha yield put on 500ml × 22,222 holes = 222 × 50kg bags
b) Organic fertiliser – with top dressing
    Woodash – 5 bags/ha
    Manure - aiming for 6-8 t/ha yield put on 350ml × 22,222 holes = 156 × 50kg bags.
    Compost - aiming for 7-9 t/ha yield put on 350ml × 22,222 holes = 156 × 50kg bags.
    Anthill soil - aiming for 1-3 t/ha yield put on 350ml × 22,222 holes = 156 × 50kg bags
    Topdressing - Urea or Ammonium Nitrate - 5 bags in total
                           - 1st topdressing heaped teaspoon (8ml) = 3 bags
                             - 2nd topdressing flat teaspoon (5ml) = 2 bags
c) Inorganic fertiliser
    Woodash – 5 bags/ha or Agricultural Lime 3 bags/ha
    Basal fertiliser - DAP or NPK - Flat tablespoon (12ml) × 22,222 holes = 293kg or 6 × 50kg bag
    Topdressing - Urea or Ammonium Nitrate - 5 bags in total
                           - 1st topdressing heaped teaspoon (8ml) = 3 bags
                            - 2nd topdressing flat teaspoon (5ml) = 2 bags

    10.2.       Making Measuring Ropes
Measuring ropes are an essential kit item for Farming God's Way. We use them to establish
permanence, high standard plant population densities and canopy cover.
For Field Scale implementation, the most commonly used rope is the 60cm measuring rope, but a
75cm measuring rope is very valuable for laying out your field as well. To make a measuring rope
use a non-stretch, hardy rope, preferably made out of a decent thickness nylon or cable as long as


                                                  68
your field but up to a maximum 50m length. When preparing bigger fields, the ropes would simply
be extended after completing each quadrant.
Tie loops on each end of the rope, peg it in on one side, then pull the rope taught and straight, and
peg it in on the other end. Either use a long tape measure or a 60cm long stick with which to
measure the marker positions on the rope. Align the centre of each marker with the measuring
tape mark or the edge of the stick and attach it firmly. When using the precut sticks simply move
the stick down the rope marking one at a time from centre to centre. The position of the first marker
relative to the corner or loop of the rope is not important, as we will always use the first and last
markers for permanence and not the corners.
Measuring rope markers can be made using:
1) 8-10 turns of soft wire wound tightly onto the taught rope, trim the tags flush with the windings
and firm with pliers. This is the preferred and easiest marker rope.
2) short plastic strips woven into the rope - 1st slacken off one end of the rope and use the 60cm
stick to get approximate positions and weave the plastic strip into the rope between the threads,
but do not tie them off yet. Once all the threads are in approximate position, reattach the loop
making sure the rope is taught and straight. Then start from the beginning again by checking the
marker positions using the stick and sliding the threaded plastic strips forwards or backwards into
the accurate 60cm position, tie them off with a double knot and trim the ends shorter.
3) bottle tops crimped onto the
rope with pliers – however these
markers can slip.
The easiest option is to make
your measuring rope when your
local Farming God's Way trainer
is with you, by laying your new
rope next to their 50m long
measuring rope and simply
attaching your markers in
alignment with the trainers’
benchmark.
Ideally, it is valuable to have
three field scale measuring
ropes. 60cm for planting stations
for many crops; 75cm for
permanence of rows; and a 45cm
measuring rope for certain
vegetable production crops.


   10.3.       Land Preparation
It is preferable to start your land preparations approximately 2 months before your planting season
begins. In the summer rainfall regions of Sub Saharan Africa start your land preparations at the
beginning of September. In central African countries like Kenya and Uganda, which experience
both long and short rains, start preparing your lands at the beginning of January for long rain
plantings.
Instead of clearing enormous areas of land, rather start small and do Farming God's Way to a high
standard. Let your available inputs that God has given you in manure, compost, anthill or fertiliser,
determine what land area to prepare.
For example: If you have only got 156 × 50kg bags of manure, don’t prepare 2 hectares, as your
total yield could be as low as 2 tons/ha or a 4 ton total, due to diluting your input volume. If you had
done 1 hectare to a high standard, your yield could be as high as 5-6 tons from 1 hectare. Instead
of diluting your inputs and increasing your costs, rather reduce your farmed area, do it to a high
standard and get better profitability, then expand in the following years.


                                                  69
a) You are most likely going to be planting into an old cropped land, so make sure the previous
crop has been knocked down and keep the land weed free throughout the off season.
b) If the land is a jungle of weeds, hoe them down to ground level, leaving them to lie as God’s
Blanket on the surface of the ground, but remove weeds that are still full of seeds. Creeping
grasses are the most problematic weeds and the most effective way to kill them is by smother
mulching the field with a thick layer of God’s blanket. If you don’t have enough blanket, then pull
out as many of the runners as you can and remove them out of the land. Keep on building your
blanket cover throughout the year to ensure you keep creeping grasses under control.
c) If you are preparing land that has never been farmed before, the soil is already in prime virgin
condition which has produced beautiful natural vegetation for centuries without ploughing or
burning. Cut down trees, destump and clear the bush, leaving God’s Blanket made up of leaves
and grasses lying on the surface of the ground. Whatever you do, at all costs, do not plough the
land or burn God’s Blanket. Level off destumped areas and spread God’s Blanket evenly over the
whole field. The forests are a part of God’s awesome wonder which we have to actively preserve.
Farming God's Way allows such drastic yield improvements that we lower land demand for
sensitive natural forests. God expects us to look after and care for His creation and by simply
increasing our yields per hectare we don’t have to destroy forested areas by using slash and burn
agriculture.

   10.4.       Field Layout
Maize and bean crops are the most commonly grown staple foods among small scale farmers in
Africa. If you do not grow these crops, follow the same principles of layout and simply adjust the
design based on the section “Alternative Crop Guidelines”.
Finding the Biggest Square
If your garden is not a square or
rectangle, and is a strange shape or
has pathways that affect its straight
edge, don’t worry because there is a
square inside every shape. Pace out
your field and find the very largest
square within the irregular shape. You
will then continue the field layout
using this largest square and at a
later stage fill in the missing pieces by
following the rows and in rows until
the entire field is prepared.
Establishing the Baseline
Always start at the highest point or topside of your field within the biggest square and place your
60cm measuring rope across the slope. Hammer in the 1st corner peg, then pull the rope taught
and straight and hammer in the 2nd corner peg. This is very important as we want to slow the water
running down the slope by planting on the contour, to limit soil erosion to an absolute minimum. A
contour is a level line that runs at 90 degrees to your slope. Where possible, establish the baseline
at 90 degrees off a road, or an existing boundary fence or pathway or wall for efficiency and
neatness; however, laying the baseline on the contour always takes priority.




                                                 70
Establishing Permanence
Hammer in permanent pegs at the 1st marker and the last marker on the upslope side of your 60cm
measuring rope.

                                        Use 20cm long pegs cut from plastic conduit, steel rods or
                                        hard wooden pegs that won’t rot. Hammer them in deeply,
                                        leaving just 5cm above the ground so they can’t be
                                        removed by animals or people. Another good technique is
                                        to fill small plastic bottles with sand, and bury them just
                                        leaving the bottle cap above the ground.
                                        These Permanent Pegs at the 1st and last marker on the
                                        baseline are very important as they guarantee that year
                                        after year, you can align the measuring rope to them and
                                        have the same starting position for permanence into
                                        perpetuity.




Holing out
The 1st technology key is “do not plough”. With single row plants planted widely apart, we simply
make holes, or planting stations, to be able to place our inputs and seed at the required depth with
as little soil disturbance as possible.
   Organic input holes
Holes for organic inputs such as manure, compost or anthill soil should be 15cm deep, 12cm
wide (as wide as a hoe head), and 15cm long.
Look up the slope and center the hoe head in
line with the marker on the 60cm measuring
rope. Start 15cm downslope from the rope,
striking deeper and deeper until you come
right up to the rope, digging to a depth of
15cm. This usually takes 5 or 6 strikes. The
hole profile is steep on the upslope side and
more gentle on the downslope side. Make a
neat soil heap downslope, being careful not to
throw the soil too far as then it will interfere
with the next 75cm row. Bringing the soil
downslope means that the water will be
slowed by the hole and then collected like a
dam and result in a deep penetration right in
the plant root zone.

   Inorganic input holes
Holes for inorganic inputs such as DAP or NPK should be just 8cm deep, 12cm wide (as wide
as a hoe head), and 10cm long. The holes for inorganic fertiliser are shallower as the input
volume is far less. Prepare the holes in the same way as described above. However, start digging
just 10cm away from the rope and complete in 3 strikes.



                                                   71
Stick to the downslope method:
1) Don’t dig the soil upslope, as the soil will be washed straight back into the hole.
2) Don’t dig the soil to the side, as the water will be moving at full pace down the slope when it hits
the heap because it doesn’t have the hole to slow it down and will have little benefit in slowing soil
movement. Side digging also results in measuring ropes being cut frequently.


Hole Spacing
The planting stations are 60cm apart in 75cm rows, which will give us 22,222 holes per hectare.
The first planting station should be dug at the 1st marker and the last one being dug at the last
marker, not at the corners. This allows for either the 75cm sticks or 75cm measuring rope to freely
move on the outside edges.




When holing out, move in an organized manner from left to right, spacing the team out evenly to
prevent wasting time in leapfrogging over one another. Inspect the planting stations after each row
to make sure that the team have dug the holes accurately at 60cm intervals, with an even depth
and size.




                                       Planting Stations across the slope

When holing out with inexperienced people, it is possible for them to cut the rope, which is then
difficult to knot to exactly 60cm intervals, so they must be careful. Another solution is to use a
“marking technique” where a surface scratch is made at each measuring rope marker across the
rope length. Once all holes are “marked”, then the rope is moved 30cm up the slope out of the way
and the holes can then be dug working from 15cm down the slope and finishing on the mark. This
protects the rope from any damaging hoe strikes, which could cut the rope.




                                                  72
Establishing the Perfect Right Angle and Corner 3
At corner 2, place a large square piece of paper in line with the baseline. Attach another rope,
either your 75cm measuring rope or an unmarked rope, to corner 2 and run it down the slope to the
bottom end of the field. With the help of an assistant, adjust the downslope rope until it lines up
exactly with the edge of the paper making a perfect right angle and hammer in corner peg 3.
You can also use the 3,4,5 method, to establish this right angle even more accurately.
Pace out the downslope length and divide your field into 3 equal portions with a mark in the soil
so you know where each of the 3 portions start. See “Land Preparation for Rotation Crops”.




Establishing the Second and Subsequent Rows
Once you have finished digging planting stations in your first row and laid out your downslope rope
in a perfect square, you will be establishing the 2nd row. Both you and your assistant must undo the
loops of the baseline rope leaving both corner pegs 1 and 2, and the downslope rope in position.
Person 1 - use a 75cm stick to measure from the centre of the corner peg 2 in line with the
downslope rope, which is your guide, and hammer in a temporary peg for row number 2. If you
have made a 75cm measuring rope, just hammer in the peg at the next marker. Attach the loop to
the temporary peg of row number 2. Do not remove the corner peg 2 at this stage as your
downslope rope square is still attached to this corner. The corner pegs will only be removed after
the completion of the entire field has been done.
Person 2 – On the opposite side to the paper square, use a 75cm stick to measure from the centre
of corner peg 1 downwards. Move the baseline rope downslope 75cm, ensuring that you lift and
drop it and pull it taught and straight. Adjust the stick and rope loop until they overlap, and hammer
in a temporary peg for row number 2. Check that your measuring rope markers line up with the
planting stations of the 1st row before digging planting stations at row 2.




                                                 73
Dig the 2nd row’s holes and then move down 75cm again using the same procedure.




Permanence After Every 10 Rows
Continue digging planting stations in each row at 60 × 75cm intervals until both of the first two
portions are completed. On a field scale, permanent pegs should be placed at the 1st and last
markers after every 10 rows (for example on the 11th, 21st, 31st rows), to ensure that you maintain
permanence. By having accurate measuring ropes and permanent pegs, a field matrix is
established which allows for planting in the same planting stations year after year. See the section
on “Permanence” in the Introduction to the six Technology Keys to gain insight into the benefits of
permanence.




                                                 74
Land Preparation for Rotation Crops
The 3rd Technology key of Farming God's Way is to “practice biodiversity” which we can
accomplish by rotating crops. When you placed the downslope rope, you divided your field into 3
equal portions. Once Portion 1 and Portion 2 have been prepared for the staple grain, maize in this
instance, begin preparing the 3rd portion with your rotation crop which should preferably be a
legume. Hammer in permanent pegs at the 1st and last marker to ensure you know where portion 3
starts for future rotations. In larger fields it is suggested that the row above a new portion be left
blank to ensure that there is a clear definition of each portion, however where farmers have limited
space this is not an option.
Example 1 – if you have permanence at the 1st, 11th, 21st rows with the last permanent pegs at the
last row 30, then portion 1 will be ten rows 1-10; portion 2 will be ten rows 11-20; and portion 3 10
rows 21-30.
Example 2 – most fields won’t have 10 row portions, so simply start each of the three portions with
permanent pegs and divide each portion equally. If you had 16 row equal portions, place pegs at
the start of each portion and divide the 16 rows into 8 rows instead of 10 rows. Portion 1 will have
permanent pegs at rows 1 and 9; start new Portion 2 at row 17 and 25; start new portion 3 at row
33 and 41 and the last set at row 48.
Example 3 – with vegetable field layout, permanence is established at each and every 75cm row.
See the “Vegetable Guide” for more information.


Rotation Planning
In year 1, Portion 1 and 2 should be planted to maize and the 3rd Portion to beans.
In year 2, Portion 1 will be planted to beans and 2 and 3 will be planted to maize.
In year 3, Portion 1 will be maize, Portion 2 will be beans and Portion 3 will be maize again.
In year 4 the rotation will revert back to how it was in the 1st year with Portion 1 and 2 to maize and
the 3rd Portion beans again.
This gives each portion a rest from the maize crop every 3 years, which allows for the breaking of
the disease cycles as well as improving soil fertility through nitrogen fixing when rotating with
legumes. Many other benefits are covered in the section on “Biodiversity through Alternative Crops
- Rotations”. Don’t be deceived into believing that you will get away with growing the same crop
year after year after year. You must have full persuasion to grow a third of your field to alternative
crops for the rotational value as well as helping to spread your market risk.
Other legumes you can grow in your 1/3rd rotation include groundnuts, soyabeans, pigeon peas or
cowpeas to name a few. You can also grow other non-legume crops such as cotton or sunflower
for your rotation. See the chapter on “Biodiversity Through Alternative Crops Section”.




                                                  75
Many of these alternative crops have smaller canopies than maize and we would not be able to
establish “Canopy Cover” if we planted them in Planting Stations. Instead, we plant them in a
continuous furrow across the slope on the contour line.
Place the measuring rope at the next 75cm row, but instead of digging planting stations at the
60cm markers simply dig a 10cm deep furrow across the slope. Repeat the furrows by measuring
with the 75cm sticks row by row until the whole of portion 3 is complete. A field with 30 rows should
have 20 rows of planting stations for maize and 10 rows of furrows for beans.




After completing all the planting stations and furrows you can remove the corner pegs leaving only
the permanent pegs in their place.




                                                 76
Field Layout Summary
Fields should be divided into 3 equal portions – 2 portions maize and 1 portion of beans in this
example. Row intervals are 75cm going down the slope with permanence established at the 1st and
last measuring rope markers. The three equal portions should then be subdivided further with
permanence not exceeding 10 rows to guarantee permanence.




Maize is planted in planting stations which are a hoe width or 12cm wide; a hand depth or 15cm
deep, when using manure, compost or anthill soil; or alternatively 8cm deep when using fertiliser;
and 60cm apart across the slope. After 2/3rd of the field has been prepared with planting stations
for maize, prepare 10cm deep furrows across the slope for beans in the remaining portion. Rotate
these portions each year.




                                                77
If you have an irregularly shaped field, complete the preparation of planting stations and furrows in
the largest square first then hyper-extend those lines one row at a time, to fill in the remaining
fringe areas as shown in the diagram below.




Remember you can’t manage what you don’t measure.
Once you have completed digging your planting stations and furrows, count and record them as
well as the number of rows per portion. These numbers are very important for costing and planning
ahead.




Look at the work of your hands and enjoy. This order that comes into what once chaos, is just like
the order that God wants to restore in our lives. Solomon wisely speaks of the joy of good hard
work and we can enjoy our garden’s beautiful matrix of in-rows, rows and if you look carefully, even
diagonals. Farmers should be able to lift up their heads and be proud of the work of their hands.


                                                 78
   10.5.       Correcting Acidic Soils
In some soil types, usually ones that are reddish in colour and where the rainfall is high, there is a
tendency for the soil to be acidic. These acidic soils bind nutrients that are necessary for plant
growth, making them unavailable to plants, hereby causing nutrient deficiencies and low yields.
Woodash, bonemeal and lime will all increase soil pH making it more neutral, which will allow for
nutrients to be more easily absorbed by the roots, which in turn means higher yields. The most
readily available resource for communities to adjust soil pH is woodash, which is typically just
thrown away. Woodash is also beneficial in that it supplies the soil with essential Potassium and
many micronutrients. Apply the woodash by placing a heaped tablespoon (15ml) along the base of
each hole or spreading it evenly across 60cm in the furrows. Bonemeal can be used as an
alternative to woodash, in the same way; however, the pH effect of bonemeal takes longer.
Bonemeal is an excellent source of phosphate, and is often just another resource that is simply
thrown away. Agricultural lime is much more basic than woodash or bonemeal, so apply a flat
teaspoon (5ml) instead of a tablespoon. Building lime is not recommended for agricultural use.
If you counted the planting stations and row length in your field, then you can calculate your pH
adjustment volume needs using the formula: holes × ml of input / 1000 * bulk density of woodash
(0.76) / 50 = numbers of 50kg bags by volume. Examples:
        1) Woodash for an acre: 10,000 holes × 15ml ÷ 1000 ÷ 50 = 2.3 bags (50kg)
        2) Woodash for a Well Watered Garden: 99 holes × 15ml ÷ 1000 *0.76 = 1.2 litres
We recommend that you apply woodash, bonemeal or lime for 3 years and thereafter do trials of
rows with and without it, to observe the visual benefits. Over the years the need to correct soil
acidity will become less and less due to the fact that we are not ploughing, increasing soil organic
matter and using 100% coverage of God’s Blanket.

   10.6.       Inputs
God has provided each farmer with a measure of talents. To some he gave 1, others 2, others 5
talents. You need to be faithful with whatever God has given you in your hand, not looking to what
you don’t have and rather looking to what you do have. We have to learn to give to get out of the
dependency syndrome. We cannot keep taking and not giving anything back. The Bible says “you
will reap what you sow”. We must change our mindset to become aligned to God’s ways!!!
Zero times zero equals zero; zero times 100 still equals zero.
If you give nothing to even good potential soil you will get nothing.
Uganda, Malawi and Mozambique have beautiful deep soils and the people’s mindset is “We don’t
need to put in any inputs as our soil is so fertile”, but in reality they are getting very low yields. We
will reap according to the measure we sow. Every season, the crop is removing nutrients from the
soil and we must give back to replenish that loss.
Organic inputs
When using manure, compost or anthill soil and
you are not going to use a top dressing, then
place 500ml in the bottom of the 15cm deep
holes.
If you are going to be using a top dressing
fertiliser, then you may decrease your basal
dressing from 500ml to 350ml.
We realise that most small scale farmers cannot
afford or get access to urea or ammonium nitrate
and so it is far better to “sow bountifully” with
500ml from the beginning. Make sure that each
hole receives the same amount of inputs so that
you get even stands that “close the canopy” early,



                                                   79
so maintain very high standards when applying inputs. Uneven stands cause non-beneficial
competition as “the worst weed in your field is another maize plant”. Place your inputs in the holes
completing 2 rows at a time to limit compaction to every second row and to save time.
If you counted the planting stations and row length in your field, then you can calculate your input
needs using the formula – holes × ml of input ÷ 1000 ÷ 50 = numbers of 50kg bags by volume.
Examples:
       1) 1 Acre using manure or compost with no top dressing: 10,000 holes × 500ml ÷ 1000 ÷ 50
       = 100 bags (50kg) or 8 scotch carts.
       2) Well Watered Garden 99 holes × 500ml = 1 bag (50kg) of manure or compost.
When placing your inputs for the bean rows, spread 500ml in the furrow every meter. With less
nutrient demanding crops such as groundnuts or cowpeas you can spread just 350ml over a meter.
Many farmers are doing it and succeeding and so can you. If you don’t have enough manure,
make compost on site…see later section on “Compost”, which is far better for your soil and results
in higher yields higher than manure.
   Manure
When using manure, “use what you have in your hand”, meaning whatever is available to you, but
try to use the freshest manure you can find, as this will have the highest nitrogen content. It is
perfectly safe to use fresh manure because of the 3cm soil separation layer applied in the next
step. Generally, a sliding scale of manure quality would start with chicken, bird, pig, horse, cattle,
goats then sheep but this also depends on what they eat. Where manure is limited rather use the
manure to make high quality Farming God's Way compost which always outperforms manure.
   Compost
When referring to Compost, that is Farming God's Way compost which is both aerobic and
thermophyllic, and made with specific ingredients and ratios. Where possible make your compost
close to the field, so it does not have to be transported a long distance.
   Anthill Soil
Anthill soil is harvested from antheaps starting with the outside edges and cutting into them with a
hoe or pick, then breaking down the clods into a crumbly texture. Anthill soil has a high clay
content, has higher potassium and organic matter content. Anthill soil is also full of a vast
biodiversity of beneficial micro-organisms, which is valuable for re-inoculating soils to bring them
back to life again.
The Farming God's Way Comparative Trials conducted over many years in Port Elizabeth, show
consistent yields of 6-8 tons/ha (160×50kg bags) with 500ml manure and no top dressing; and 7-9
tons/ha (180×50kg bags) with 500ml compost with no top dressing; with small scale Farming God's
Way farmers achieving up to 3 tons/ha (60×50kg bags) with just anthill soil. The yield average in
the African continent amongst small scale farmers is around 0.35 tons/ha which is just 7×50kg
bags in Southern African countries and just 15 bags in the more tropical Central Regions, and so
we can comfortably go more than ten times better using these simple proven and available
solutions. The 3 organic input types we encourage, all build up our soil microbial life and fertility,
improve texture, structure and the soil’s ability to hold moisture and are much more preferable and
cost effective to use than fertiliser.

Inorganic fertiliser inputs
Our primary focus is on the extension of organic input types and most small scale farmers have no
access to inorganic fertiliser because of its high cost and the remoteness of their areas, but they do
sometimes get given fertiliser vouchers or bags and should therefore know how to use it wisely. In
many African countries people are scared of using them, having been given false information. Like
with most things, it is not its use but its abuse, which will have a negative impact on the soil.
Remember, in large scale commercial farming systems it is nearly impossible to give back the
volumes of nutrients removed by the crop and get very high yields and so there is a place for
fertiliser.



                                                  80
Fertilisers come in 2 types; basal and topdressing.
The basal types include DAP or Diammonium phosphate and NPK in varying ratio’s. They have a
balance of nutrients and are made up of nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium. Basal fertilisers get
placed in the bottom of the holes.
Topdressing types have mostly got nitrogen in them. They get buried just below the ground surface
and include CAN or calcium ammonium nitrate and Urea. For optimal yields place a flat tablespoon
or 12ml of DAP in a narrow band across the bottom of each hole.
An example to calculate your inorganic fertiliser requirement is:
       12ml × 22,222 holes = 293kg or 6 bags of DAP per hectare. The record Farming God's
       Way yield was 13.9 tons per hectare on large commercial scale using fertiliser.
If you are using inorganic fertiliser for your bean rotation crop, simply spread a heaped teaspoon or
8ml evenly every meter in the furrow.
Only prepare land areas that you can put correct inputs into and don’t be tempted to spread your
inputs thinly over a larger area. If you only have a limited amount of inputs, rather use what you
have on a smaller area and do it to a high standard, than doing a large portion to a low standard.

   10.7.       Covering to Seeding Depth
Cover all the inputs with a 3cm soil separation layer, using soil from the downslope soil heap. This
separation layer is very important to prevent the seed from being burnt by the inputs, especially
when using fertiliser or manure. The separation layer also allows you to get the exact planting
depth, which for maize is 5cm or a matchbox length.
Your bean rows need a final planting depth of 3cm deep (matchbox on its side) so cover leaving
this depth remaining.
Covering the inputs with this 3cm soil separation layer also prevents the loss of nitrogen to the
atmosphere through volatilization and so it is important to complete covering inputs as a part of
your “Plant Readiness” preparations before the onset of the rains.

   10.8.       Waiting for the Rains – Plant Readiness
No matter where you are in the world, your field should be ready for planting at least 3 weeks
before optimal planting time. This way you are sure of being on time, especially if you have good
early rains.
In the summer rainfall regions such as South Africa, Zambia, Zimbabwe, Mozambique and Malawi
you should be at “Plant Readiness” by the end of October, having dug your holes and placed your
inputs and covered with 3cm of soil, in readiness for the 10th-25th November optimal planting date.
The table below displays a guideline for the amount of rain you should receive before planting in
Southern Africa.
                    Total Amount of Rainfall                 Planting Date
                           Received
                            100 mm                    1st week November
                              85 mm                   2nd week November
                              75 mm                   3rd week November
                              50 mm                   4th week November
                              30 mm                   1st week December


In the central region where the long rains of Kenya, Tanzania, Rwanda and Uganda are dominant,
you should have ‘Plant Readiness” by the end of February in readiness for mid-March planting.



                                                 81
We strongly recommend that farmers do not practise dry planting, which involves the planting of
seeds before the rains actually come. If you have early rains that are just sufficient for germination
and then have a dry spell, you will have to replant, wasting seed, time and effort. You must wait
for sufficient rains before planting.

   10.9.       Planting
We advocate that small scale farmers use open pollinated variety seed (OPV’s) as opposed to
genetically modified (GMO) or hybrid seed. They perform at much the same yield under sub
optimal conditions, are much cheaper, and the same seed can be stored at the end of the season
and replanted for many years. OPV’s can be stored simply and effectively by first getting them to
the correct moisture content, then mixing woodash with the seed at a 10% ratio and storing in bags
in a cool, dry place. This prevents both fungal and insect post harvest storage problems.
We heartily discourage the planting of GMO and Hybrid seed, which must be bought each year.
The poor get yoked into government and seed companies’ schemes with these seed types and
usually become seed aid dependents. The poor should be free to plant when they are ready to
plant and not yoked to men, governments or organizations.
If you have to buy in seed, then buy the best open pollinated variety that you can afford in the 1st
season. When you are ready to plant and have had sufficient rainfall, take the amount of maize
seed you can plant at one time and presoak it for up to 6 hours to allow the seed to take up water
and swell.
Looking up the slope, place 3 seeds per planting station neatly in the row, one on the left, one in
the middle and one on the right. 3 seeds per hole is 66,666 seeds planted per hectare. The seeds
are planted at 5cm deep or a matchbox length upright. If the hole is too full of soil then simply
press the seed to the required depth (from the ground level) with your finger.
When planting in organic input holes with manure, compost or anthill, the seeds should be put 5cm
away from the topside of the holes, as that is the deepest part of the 15cm hole where most
nutrients are found. When planting in fertiliser holes, place the seeds 2,5cm or a matchbox width
away, as the deepest part of the hole is much closer to the top edge of the 8cm deep holes.
When planting beans, place the bean seed at 10cm intervals along the row.
These are all part of the standards that are going to give God glory in our fields.

   10.10.      Covering the Seed
Cover the seed with fine, loose soil from the soil heap, making sure that you don’t cover them with
stones, heavy clods or God’s Blanket. All of these can result in poor germination rates and uneven
stands, so take good care at this point. Cover evenly with no depressions. These depressions can
cause problems as pools of water develop here after heavy rains and seedlings could get diseases
such as damping off as their stems will be right in the water.

   10.11.      Applying God’s Blanket
If you go to God’s garden, which is anywhere where there is no intervention by man, you will see
that He in His wisdom puts a protective covering on top of the ground made up of leaves, twigs,
fruit and decaying grasses. These lie on top of the ground, gradually decomposing over many
years. We call this mulch God’s Blanket!!!
It protects the soil from erosion, allows for deep penetration of rainfall, conservation of moisture,
improves fertility, crops have improved drought tolerance and an ideal growing environment,
amongst many other advantages. All this leads to significantly improved yields. Aim for 100% cover
which is 2.5cm thick in your fields and although it may take you a few years to get there, do this
with all your heart.
In the beginning your only plant material available for God’s Blanket is weeds and old crop
residues. Do whatever you can to increase your blanket coverage by cutting grasses or banana


                                                  82
leaves, carrying in forest mulch or even collecting your neighbour’s crop residues before they get
burnt. Cover the whole field evenly even over the maize planting stations. The maize seeds will
germinate right through the blanket, so don’t fear.
When planting all other seeded crops, you should take care to leave a narrow 5cm space over the
seeded rows to allow for germination, after which the blanket can be brought right around the plant
bases. When planting vegetable seedlings first apply the 2.5cm thick blanket and then use a dibble
stick to make a hole and plant. See the “Vegetable Guide” for more on this technique.
God’s Blanket is so important it cannot be emphasized enough. Nobody would simply allow a thief
to walk in in broad daylight and steal from their homes would they? Why would you allow the most
important aspect of your farming practise to be burnt or eaten by your neighbour’s animals?
We need to see God’s Blanket as “white gold”, the treasure of the soil, so guard it and build it up.

   10.12.       Emergence


After approximately 7-10 days the maize
seedlings will emerge through God’s Blanket. The
seedlings usually emerge slightly slower through
God’s Blanket due to cooler soil temperatures, but
don’t panic, as they soon catch up and overtake
seedlings in fields where the blanket is not
present. It is not how you start the race which
counts, but how you finish.




   10.13.       Gapping
Sometimes chickens, guineafowl or rats dig out the seed causing fairly large empty spaces, which
we can correct by gapping. This is done shortly after emergence. Gapping of maize is only done
when you have several empty planting stations and not when one is empty.
Take the amount of maize seed you need and presoak it for 6 hours to allow it to take up water and
swell. Push the seeds down to 5cm or matchbox length into existing holes and close them over.
Gapping fills any empty spaces in your field to ensure homogenous stands and good canopy cover
so be on time with this step.

   10.14.       1st Weeding
Weeds significantly lower crop yields because they compete with plants for water, nutrients, light
and space. Weeds are stimulated to germinate by disturbance of the soil, scratching of the seed
coat and exposure to sunlight.
Farming God’s Way counteracts these stimuli by:
               not ploughing and only having a minimal soil disturbance
               100% cover with God’s Blanket 2.5cm thick which minimises exposure to sunlight
               Planting at 60×75cm spacing, which effectively cuts out light by closing the canopy
                from as early as 5 weeks, hereby reducing weed seed exposure to sunlight.
If you have collected and stored supplementary blanket, then put it down when the seedlings are
around 20-30cm tall to get 100% cover and 2.5cm thick. If you do this step, you will not need to do
any weeding throughout the growing cycle. By way of example, we have not weeded our
comparative gardens for 5 years.




                                                 83
Weeding is an enormous cost to farmers and along with ploughing costs they can even make
farming unviable and so you are encouraged to stop weeding altogether and use God’s blanket
and cover cropping techniques in the off season instead. These are not only beneficial for weed
control, but have many other values too. See the chapter “20 Reasons why we do the how”.
If your blanket is insufficient or has decomposed to become like a sheet, then do your first weeding
when the weeds are still small (around 3cm tall) which is usually 10-14 days after planting. When
weeds are weeded out at this “white hair stage” they are very easy to cut off and cannot re-grow.
Weed by walking backwards, simply hoeing them off at the soil surface and leaving them to add to
God’s Blanket. Pull out creeping grasses and carry them out of your field, but try to disturb as little
soil as possible. Repeat the weeding process “on time” every 10-14 days.


Example:
A Farming God's Way farmer, weeding when the weeds are just 3cm tall, will complete a hectare
by working less than 5 hours a day for 7 days.
The lazy farmer says “I’ll do this weeding tomorrow”. He waits for the weeds to get to 30cm tall
and because they are so big and the job so much more difficult, it will take him 14 days to do the
same hectare. which is twice as long. The weed cycle is a 10 day cycle, so the weeds will be 4
days bigger than when he first started. He will always be behind with his weeding, lose his joy and
have a significantly lower yield.
Sin and weeds have a lot in common. When you are tempted to sin and you deal with it at the
temptation stage, it cannot bear bad fruit, but if you leave temptation unattended for just a few
moments, its fruit will leave you in disgrace. In the same way, weed your fields when the weeds are
tiny and never let weeds set seed in your field, as they produce hundreds of thousands of seeds,
which can remain dormant in the soil for 20 years, waiting for the right conditions for germination.
Just one year of poor weed control can result in 7 years of weed problems.
Weed control is a major contributor to crop losses and even failure and many farmers rarely
understand just how important it really is. Keep your lands weed free throughout the year by being
“On time”, doing it to a “High standard” which will result in “Minimal wastage” of your time and effort
and make a big difference to your sustainable profitability.




                                                  84
   10.15.      Thinning
Maize achieves optimally at 44,444 plants per hectare in most cases and to achieve this with
22,222 planting stations/ha we should only have 2 plants/planting stations. So why did we plant 3
when we actually only want two?
Seed is only certified to around 87 percent germination, so if we had planted 44,444 seeds, only
about 38,000 would have come up, excluding losses from rats, chicken or guineafowl, leaving
probably 35,000 plants per hectare. Because of this it is impossible to get our optimal yield
potential unless we plant 3 seeds per hole and thin down to 2. It has been proven that for every
cent you spend on the 3rd seed you will get back 40 cents through the thinning process. That is a
40 times cost to benefit ratio – so it’s worth it.
Thin at 2-3 weeks after emergence when seedlings are around 20 to 30cm tall.
You do this by uprooting the weakest plant, or if they are all the same size then the middle plant of
each hole’s emerged seedlings. Look at 3 holes at a time, not one, and thin to 6 seedlings per 3
planting stations.
Thinning example 1 – 9 seedlings per 3 holes, so thin 1 from each hole.




   Remove middle                          Remove weakest                        Remove weakest


Thinning example 2 – 7 seedlings per 3 holes, so thin 1 from the hole with 3 in it.




                                           Remove weakest/middle


Thinning example 3 – 6 seedlings per 3 holes, so leave all.




   10.16.      1st Topdress
Dry manure, compost or anthill soil are not useful for topdressing as the Nitrogen is released into
plant useable form too late to be of benefit to the current crops growth. Rather apply these dry
inputs as a basal dressing in 500ml volumes at the beginning, then you do not need to apply either
of the top dressings. Our trials have proven that small scale farmers can excel by increasing the
basal dressing to 500ml and not applying any top dressing. See the yield data from earlier.
If you applied a basal inorganic fertiliser or just 350ml of organic inputs and can afford it, then do
your top dressing immediately after thinning, at about 2-3 weeks after emergence.
Options for top dressing include:
        1) Urine - On a small scale you can use urine, which has proven effective at increasing
yields. However, the vast quantities required and the storage requirements prior to use, prevent
urine from becoming a major topdress option for the poor. Collected urine should also stand for a



                                                   85
minimum of 1 month to be safe for use. Apply 350ml on the upslope side and one hand width away
from each planting station several times before tasselling. Be careful to avoid contact of the liquid
topdressing with stems or leaves. One litre of urine is equivalent to 5ml of AN topdressing.
         2) Manure tea – make a liquid form of manure by adding a spadeful of fresh manure to a 10
litre bucket of water and stir it until it is a dark colour. Apply 350ml on the upslope side and one
hand width away from each planting stations several times before tasselling, being careful to avoid
contact with stems or leaves.
         3) Ammonium Nitrate or Urea fertiliser – Apply the topdressing while working in pairs. The
first person makes the small 3cm deep holes with a sharp stick, one hand width away on the
upslope side of each planting station. The hole position is very important to avoid burning the
seedlings. The second person follows closely behind, placing a heaped teaspoon (8ml) of Urea or
LAN in each of the prepared holes, then uses their foot to close the small hole before moving to the
next one. Working in pairs makes this step very quick and accurate. This method prevents nitrogen
losses to the atmosphere as the top dressing is in the ground and covered. There must be good
residual soil moisture or rainfall for the topdressing to become available to the plant.

   10.17.      2nd and 3rd Weeding
It is always preferable to continue to build up the blanket coverage and smother mulch small
weeds throughout the growing season instead of weeding and it is so rewarding to see the
reduction in weed growth when you have a good blanket in place. If you need to weed, then do so
every 10-14 days, weeding backwards when the weeds are still tiny at 3cm tall. You will most likely
have to do 2 or 3 weedings before full canopy stage if you don’t have a “High standard” blanket in
place.

   10.18.      Full Canopy
At about 6 weeks your plants will be about waist high and because of the 60 by 75cm spacing, they
will close the canopy completely. This allows the leaves to capture the maximum amount of
sunlight for good growth; shades out weeds; establishes an ideal environment for good competition
between plants; and creates a productive microclimate inside the canopy.

   10.19.      Desuckering
Suckers sometimes branch off the parent plant at ground level, especially when there is a lot of
nitrogen available to them. Some advisors say they should be removed, but surprisingly this has
not proven to make any difference to the yield. If you want to do desuckering for neatness sake,
simply snap off the suckers with a quick motion before your second topdress.

   10.20.      2nd Topdressing
If you applied 500ml of organic inputs as a basal dressing, then it is not essential to topdress.
However, it is recommended if you applied a basal inorganic fertiliser or just 350ml of organic
inputs and you can afford it.
The 2nd topdressing should be applied at the piping stage, when the maize just starts to show the
tassel coming through the top of the funnel, usually at around 8 weeks after emergence. Apply 5ml
or a flat teaspoonful of either Ammonium Nitrate or Urea. First make a small 3cm deep hole with a
stick, one hand width (10cm) on the upslope side of each planting station to ensure you don’t burn
the plants. Then place the teaspoonful in the hole and cover it with soil using your foot to prevent
nitrogen loss to the atmosphere.




                                                 86
   10.21.      Pollination and Seed Fill
In a maize plant there is an interesting biological phenomenon, where every single ovule has an
individual silk, the long golden brown coloured thread which comes through the top of the cob.
During the pollination phase of the plant, a single pollen grain lands on the silk and grows down the
silk until it comes into contact with the ovule, where it fertilises to form a seed. The silks which
attach to the bottom rows of ovules emerge first, and therefore get pollinated first and the maize
cob will gradually fill going upwards until the topmost seeds are filled. However, the silks need to
be moist in order for pollination to take place, otherwise the pollen cannot grow through the silk’s
tube. If the silks dry off through insufficient available moisture during this stage of the crop then
significant crop losses can occur. So moisture conservation or supply through irrigation is critical
during the pollination phase.

   10.22.      Pre-harvest - Cover Cropping, Relay Cropping or Late Weeding
The maize plant is usually physiologically mature between 90-120 days and then begins to dry off
and die back. The leaves fold up and for the first time since canopy closure, light will penetrate
through the canopy.
If there is some rainfall at this drying off stage or enough residual soil moisture, then it is highly
recommended to plant a cover crop at this time. They can be sown by broadcasting suitable cover
crop seed under the canopy, after which a light shaking of the blanket, or surface weeding will
allow for there to be sufficient cover crop seed-to-soil contact and germination. Cover crops have
many benefits such as weed control, nitrogen fixation with legumes, soil improvements and
biodiversity to name a few. See the section “Alternative Crops - Cover Crops” for more information.
Many Farming God's Way farmers capitalize on the residual moisture by planting relay crops like
cowpeas or pigeon peas at this stage. The relay crops do really well because of the shade benefits
early on in their growth cycle. At harvest time these relay crops will already be around 30cm tall
and strong enough to manage the disturbance of post-harvest stalk lodging. These Relay Crops
can be planted because of the conservation of the moisture under God’s blanket and allow farmers
to gain a 2nd harvest in a season where they would typically only have received one harvest. Relay
crops also have many benefits besides a second harvest, including weed control, nitrogen fixation
with legumes, biodiversity and the right to protect against animals grazing, however they do not
have the same long term soil benefits as cover crops as there is a net extraction of nutrients out of
the soil. See the section “Alternative Crops - Relay Cropping”.
If there is insufficient blanket cover after physiological maturity, then the sunlight penetrating
through the canopy will stimulate weed growth as there is still some residual moisture in the soil.
Most farmers make the mistake of leaving this last weeding and they end up harvesting in a jungle
of weeds like black jacks and burrs, that stick in their clothes or buffalo beans that burn their skin.
The harvest, which is supposed to be a joyful experience, is now a misery. A light weeding at this
time will allow for the harvest to be done with joy and also ensure a weed free stand in the off
season.

   10.23.       Harvest Time
Harvest when your maize is physiologically mature, usually 2 months after tasselling. The stalks
will look very dry and the cobs will most probably be hanging down. The seeds will be well dented,
glassy and too hard to scratch with your nail. At this stage the maize is at about 30% moisture
content. Harvest your cobs and store them in a safe, dry, sunny place around your house, on the
roof top or on a drying rack, until they are properly dried out. The seeds will have no transparent
sections in them when ready to store - that is at about 13% moisture content. Only then should the
maize be shelled, dusted with woodash or laced with neem leaves or storage tablets, and bagged.




                                                  87
   10.24.      Post Harvest Stalk Lodging
Post-harvest stalk lodging is not commonly done, instead the stalks are traditionally left standing
upright in the fields or grazed shorter by cattle. This is the single most important cause of the stalk
borer infestations in maize fields being so high. Stalk borer is responsible for around 25% of the
yield losses in maize fields annually, and yet we can control it if we understand its lifecycle.
Stalk borer starts off as a pupa at the start of each season, it then becomes a moth which lays
eggs on the stems. The eggs hatch into worms, which bore through the stems, leaving their
trademark breathing holes on the sides of the stalks. They then pupate after 27 days and the whole
cycle starts again. This cycle happens several times during the growing season of the maize plant,
but at the end of the season the pupae form at the crown of the plant, on the coolest shady side at
the soil surface. If we knock down the maize stalks, we expose the pupae to UV radiation and heat.
This dries them out and along with the opportunistic feeding of other species such as ants, birds
and decomposing bacteria, this simple practice significantly reduces stalk borer infestation.
Interestingly enough, grazing alone has proven to be inadequate in the reduction of stalk borer
incidence, as the crown is still left intact. This stresses the importance of not cutting or grazing
down the stalks but rather knocking them down completely.
Knock the stalks down backwards, by kicking them off at the crown, laying them neatly in the inter
rows at about 30 degrees to the row, so they can be easily worked when doing land preparations
next season. These stalks are our new blanket or mulch, so don’t burn them and do your best to
protect them from animals. Next season we will plant into these maize stalks, which would have
decomposed quite significantly.
