                                                         90
11. Well Watered Garden (Trainer Content)

Farming God's Way is the gospel of Jesus Christ with boots on!!! It is a gospel of both Word and
deed and it is at the Well Watered Garden that we get down and dirty, humbling ourselves, and
practically serving and equipping the poor to come out of poverty.

   11.1.       What is a Well Watered Garden?
Well Watered Gardens are small 6×6m demonstration gardens, which allow for the training and
discipleship of farmers in their own communities. Although they are small they are still large
enough to train farmers how to implement the Farming God's Way techniques successfully on their
own large farms. They take 2-3 hours to complete and are very cost effective models.
It is important to note that we are not trying to teach Well Watered Gardens to community
members, but rather to teach them how to do Farming God's Way at a Well Watered Garden
training site. Over the past 20 years of extension it has been noted that there are farmers who say
they are doing Farming God's Way but in reality have only planted a Well Watered Garden - this is
simply deception. If a farmer is starting out with Farming God's Way, a trial plot for a year is fine
but thereafter they should be implementing on Field Scale. So be aware of this and communicate
field scale principles throughout Well Watered Garden training sessions and also serve adopting
farmers with field scale layout and farm visits.
Community training sites
Whole communities can gather around these Well Watered Gardens, not only at planting time, but
throughout the cropping cycle so farmers can learn the step by step procedures from hole
preparation, fertilisation, planting, topdressing, weeding and harvesting. Be encouraged to make
the Well Watered Garden experience fun by using competitions and drama and give the farmers
lots of hands on opportunities.
Timing
The best time to put in your Well Watered Garden is long before the rainy season, so that farmers
can adopt what they have learnt and still have sufficient time to prepare their fields in the same
way. For example in an area where planting is done at the end of November, which is typical in the
summer rainfall region of Southern Africa, the ideal time to put in your Well Watered Gardens is in
September, leaving the farmers with 2 months to prepare their lands so they will be ready for
planting on time. If you leave it too late, then they will have already ploughed.
However, if you want to plant Well Watered Garden trial plots instead of training plots, then plant
them at the optimal time in your region.
Segmentation
If you are living nearby to the community you are serving, you have a great advantage. Break the
teaching into sections and then get your group of farmers to complete each section before they get
their next bite size chunk of training.
Biblical incorporation
Well Watered Gardens are great opportunities for the preaching of the gospel, so remember to
integrate the Word into your teaching at frequent intervals by using parables and stories, which will
reflect the gospel applied to life.
Commitment
“Like springs of water that never fail” be committed to serve the poor faithfully, repeating the
training for several years. We feel very strongly about this commitment, which establishes a
relational platform on which to build and also brings the community adoption of Farming God's
Way to account. In general, very few farmers are brave enough to change immediately, with some
communities showing a much slower adoption rate than others, often a couple of years, yet
through perseverance, wholeheartedness, and committed service, God suddenly gives the
breakthrough and the number of followers swell the adoption rates significantly. So don’t give up




                                                 91
loving and serving, not in the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th or even 5th year. For His breakthrough, He needs your
commitment.

   11.2.       Characteristics of a Good Well Watered Garden Site
Here are some pointers to help you choose the best Well Watered Garden site:
Choose a prominent and accessible place where the whole community can see what you are
doing. Remember, you will be planting at the same site for many years, so select the correct site
right from the start.
         Matthew 5:14 “Jesus said, ‘You are the light of the world. A city set on a hill cannot be
         hidden. Nor do people light a lamp and put it under a basket, but on a stand, and it gives
         light to all in the house. In the same way, let your light shine before others, so that they may
         see your good works and give glory to your Father who is in heaven.”
Good sites are at entrances to community centres, churches or at crossroads.
Make sure you have a large enough area before you start, preferably 10×10m, and square it off
with existing structure. Fence in the site to protect it from animals by using available materials like
bamboo or palm branches or thorn branches, or grow living fences. The actual garden size is only
6 × 6m but we want to allow enough room for people to be able to come into the fenced area, so
the 2m perimeter is important.
Shade drastically reduces crop yield, so ensure the site that you choose has no shade throughout
the day.
Because Well Watered Gardens are planted before the rains, choose a site which is close to
water, so that the garden can be watered by hand until the rains come.
Choose a faithful steward to be your site manager who is close at hand to care for the garden.
Standards - we want Well Watered Gardens to be a light in the darkness so do everything at
your site to a high standard that will give God glory.

   11.3.       Measuring Ropes for Trainers
The Well Watered Garden is 6m wide and 6m long and will have 11 planting stations across the
slope and 9 rows down the slope, so you will need to make two measuring ropes for your Well
Watered Garden Kit:
       1) the first rope is 7.2m long with eleven 60cm markers on it;
       2) the second rope is 6m long and will give your garden 9 rows, with seven 75cm markers
       and the 2 loop ends making up the other two rows.
Use a non-stretch, hardy rope, preferably made out of nylon or cable. Cable is obviously more
hardy and it is nearly impossible for trainees to cut it by mistake at the Well Watered Garden. If you
use a nylon rope it should be 3-5mm in diameter, to prevent it from being cut easily. Tie loops on
each end of the rope, peg it in on one side, then pull the rope taught and straight, and peg it in on
the other end. Use a tape measure or 60cm stick to accurately attach markers at exactly 60cm
intervals along the 7.2m long ropes. Repeat the same procedure with a tape measure or 75cm
stick to accurately attach markers at exactly 75cm intervals along the 6m long rope. The best is to
use narrow gauge cable with crimps squashed onto the cable with pliers or a hammer; alternatively
wrap 8-10 turns of soft wire onto the cable/rope; or weave short strips of plastic into the rope, tie
them off with a double knot and trim them 1cm in length.
During your training sessions you must take the time to assist the Farming God's Way community
members to make up their own long measuring ropes and sticks. So trainers should also carry field
scale measuring ropes and precut sticks:
       1) one 50m measuring rope with 60cm markers on it for planting stations;
       2) the second 50m measuring rope with 75cm markers on it for permanence of rows;
       3) and for those working with vegetables a 50m measuring rope with 45cm markers on it for
       certain vegetable crops.



                                                      92
                              The most commonly used rope is the 60cm marker rope. The best
                              way to make measuring ropes is to lay out a 50m long high standard
                              field scale rope on a level piece of ground, and peg it in on either
                              side making sure it is taught and straight. Next hammer in nails or
                              pegs a few centimeters away from your measuring rope’s pegs, so
                              that the community members can lay their ropes next to your
                              baseline, and let them then simply copy the markers using the
                              corresponding markers on your measuring rope. They can use soft
                              wire, plastic inserts or even bottle tops although these are prone to
                              slipping. Your field scale ropes will allow many, many, measuring
                              ropes to be made by the farmers so yours needs to be made to
                              excellence. The farmers can make ropes out of whatever they have
                              in their hand including nylon, material tape, woven grass braid, inner
                              linings of tyres and even stiff wire in segments. It is however
                              essential to make the best possible rope with the longest lasting,
                              non-stretch material that they can as these will be used for many
                              years.



Items you will need in order to be able to make field scale ropes in communities include:
          50m measuring ropes with 60cm markers
          50m measuring ropes with 75cm markers
          Blank nylon rope
          Nails as many as the ropes you will be making
          Plastic bags cut into lots of 1cm wide and 10cm long strips
          Soft binding or copper wire
          Several sets of pliers
It is also very important to cut sticks with the community members as they should each leave the
training with one 60cm and two 75cm sticks and a measuring rope.

   11.4.       Well Watered Garden Trainer Kit List
What you need to take with you:
              7.2m measuring rope with 60cm markers
              6m measuring rope with 75cm markers
              Downslope rope without markers
              Large Paper Square
              4 permanent pegs
              25 × 4 inch nails
              1 × matchbox
              2 × 75cm sticks
              1 × 60cm stick
              3 × teaspoons (5ml flat or 8ml heaped)
              3 × tablespoons (12ml flat or 15ml heaped)
              3 × 500ml cups or tins
              1 Cup of good quality maize seed about 350 seeds
              1 Cup of good quality bean seed about 180 seeds
If you have space include:
              Hammer
              1.5 litres of woodash
              0.5kg of basal fertiliser - Compound D, DAP or NPK 2.3.2.
              0.5kg of topdress fertiliser - AN or Urea


                                                93
Most small scale farmers do not use fertiliser, so we will focus on the use of inputs according to
God’s All Sufficiency – Manure, Compost and Anthill Soil. However, it is useful to have some
fertiliser on hand, to show the community how to use it effectively and demonstrate that in the 6th
row.
Get the following items on site:
              Five hoes
              One 50kg bag of manure
              One packet of woodash
              A large heap of God’s blanket, made up of plant materials such as leaves, thatch
               grass or maize stalks.
              200 litres of water
See the Appendix for a printable checklist.

   11.5.       Well Watered Garden Pre-Training Layout
Remember we are not trying to teach the poor how to put in Well Watered Gardens, but rather how
to do Farming God's Way in their fields. The best way to do this is to lay out your Well Watered
Garden before the community arrives. The clearing and levelling takes a half an hour with a few
helpers, depending on the weeds, grasses and levels, but the actual layout of corners, square and
side pegs, should take you less than 5 minutes and allow you to have your garden setup to flow
easily thereafter.

Clear and Level
This preparation takes a little time but with a few helpers can be done very quickly before the
layout. Square off your garden with a wall, road or pathway. Clear the weeds in the garden area by
cutting them off at the surface and pull up creeping grass as best you can. Try to get the Well
Watered Garden to be as level as possible. If you know that you will be training in a new garden
area then a month or two before the time lay a thick blanket on top of the ground, to kill any
creeping grasses and soften the soil for ease of digging the planting stations or furrows.
The blanket should always be on the Well Watered Garden, not taken off to plant and on again
afterwards. It is important to do this, so you can demonstrate field scale blanket management. The
blanket should be 2.5cm or less at the start, don’t make it too thick as then it will be difficult for the
learners to work. After completing the planting, add supplementary blanket and encourage farmers
to do the same instead of weeding.

Baseline, Corner 1 and 2
Place the 7.2m long 60cm measuring rope across the slope on the contour. Hammer in the 1st nail
at corner 1 and place the loop onto it, then place the 2nd nail into the other loop and pull the rope
taught and straight and hammer in the 2nd nail at corner 2. The 7.2m measuring rope with 60cm
markers on it going across the slope is your baseline. Do not put in permanent pegs at 1st and last
markers yet as you will teach this during the training in due course.

Corner 3
Connect the loop of your 6m long 75cm measuring rope to the nail at corner 2 and lay it down the
slope. Place a large piece of paper onto your baseline and using this as a guide adjust the 75cm
rope until it makes a 90 degree angle off the baseline. Pull the rope taught and straight and
hammer in the nail at corner 3. You now have an L shape to your garden.
Place nails on the outside edge of each of the 75cm markers on your measuring rope. There will
be a nail at each end of the 9 rows going down the slope. Dig marker holes 5cm deep on the
outside of each of these nails as sometimes the nails pull out or they are stepped on, when
learners are helping in the garden. If this happens, these marker holes will help you to find the nail
placements again and allow you to replace the rope in the right spot again.


                                                   94
Corner 4
Remove the loops of all the measuring ropes but leave all the nails in their place. Move the 7.2m
long measuring rope to the bottom of the garden and connect a loop to corner 3. Move the 6m long
downslope measuring rope to the other side of the garden and connect a loop to corner 1. Pull the
two ropes towards the prospective corner 4 and place a nail in the loops of both ropes, pull them
taught and straight and then hammer in the nail at corner 4. Your garden should now be perfectly
square. Again place the remaining 7 nails on the outside edge of each of the marks of your 75cm
measuring rope and dig marker holes next to each of these nails, giving you the 9 rows on the
other side.
Bring your 60cm baseline back to the top of your garden and connect it back on to the baseline
nails at corners 1 and 2. You can use your 75cm downslope rope later if you want to encourage
them to make a 75cm rope, however experience shows they rarely do so. Instead they choose to
use the rotation of the 75cm sticks off a blank downslope rope, so this must be taught at the Well
Watered Garden.
Lay the Well Watered Garden out correctly from the start as this will allow you to plant in the same
permanent holes for all the years you serve there. Your layout of corners and nails will be used as
your guidelines to make sure your Well Watered Garden flows smoothly. Your kit should be laid out
neatly and in order of use, about 2m on the upside of your garden edge to allow everyone to see
what they will need for their implementation.




You are now ready to welcome the trainees to the garden to teach Farming God's Way with your
Well Watered Garden marked out before the training starts.




                                                95
   11.6.       Well Watered Garden Trainer Tips

Step by Step Procedure
From here onwards simply follow the step by step procedure starting with land preparation;
clearing and levelling; and working your way through all the steps until post harvest stalk lodging.

Well Watered Garden Permanence
Permanence is established in row 1 at the 1st and last marker on the measuring rope after
teaching about laying down the baseline across the slope. Once you get to row 9, which is the last
Well Watered Garden row, establish permanence there once again at the 1st and last marker on
the measuring rope. Although this is at the 9th row, encourage farmers to place them after every 10
rows or at equal numbers in their portions. This is very important for permanence and planning.

Well Watered Garden Inputs
Before you put in your Well Watered Garden decide on your main input type - manure, compost,
anthill soil or inorganic fertiliser. Find out which is the most commonly used and available in that
community. Well Watered Garden sites usually have very poor soils and are seldom top dressed,
so it is recommended that you use 500ml bowls for organic inputs instead of 350ml bowls. In most
cases fresh manure will be the main input, but composting will be the way of the future, so get
those compost piles cooking. You will most likely be demonstrating how to use manure as an input
in planting stations for the maize stands, but if you are in an area where inorganic fertiliser, like
DAP or NPK, is used then demonstrate that in the 6th row to show how to use inorganic fertiliser
safely.
With the bean crop, place 500ml of manure in the furrows every meter, which is very close to the
previous recommendation of 350ml/60cm.
Planting stations and furrows are often dug too shallow at Well Watered Gardens, so ensure you
inspect every hole and furrow before allowing inputs to be put down. If this is not done at a high
standard then there is a high risk of seed burn, which will make your Well Watered Garden a poor
demonstration of Farming God's Way in the community.

Rotations
Trainers must demonstrate and teach “biodiversity through rotations” at every Well Watered
Garden opportunity as this is a very important part of farmers becoming sustainably profitable.
Well Watered Gardens have 9 rows, so in the 1st year plant the 1st and 2nd portions with maize in
rows 1-6, and the 3rd portion with beans in furrows in rows 7-9. In year 2 the planting of the 3 bean
rows will be in rows 1-3 and in year 3 the bean rows will rotate into rows 4-6. You will be
questioned about permanence when changing from planting stations to furrows and it is simply
answered by permanence is secured in planting stations for two years out of three and in the
rotation year the furrows provide permanence within the same planting line.




                                                 96
                                Completed Well Watered Garden Layout
Irrigation
The training at the Well Watered Garden must be done well before the rains come in order for the
farmers to have enough time for field scale land preparations. This usually means that it will need
to be watered at the time of planting and at least weekly thereafter until the rains start.
Apply 2 litres to each planting station before planting, which means you will need 200 litres of water
on hand. This watering can really slow down your garden timing, so ensure you are well prepared
so this doesn’t cause any delays. Repeat waterings once or twice a week up until the rains begin.
It must be clearly understood by the farmers that this is simply for demonstrating the techniques
before the rains come.
In extreme cases where there is no option to water or care for the Well Watered Garden, then
simply use it as a training garden and not to actually produce a crop. When a garden is planted
without water, the plants must be uprooted straight after germination as the crop will not be a good
advertisement for Farming God's Way. In both cases it is essential to discourage farmers from “dry
planting” which is very risky and wasteful.

Thinning
When illustrating thinning it is best to cut 20 × 20cm long sticks and place them in planting stations
in random groups of 3, 2, 1 or zero to simulate the thinning technique.

Crowd Control
Well Watered Gardens are probably the best but also the most difficult classrooms you will
encounter. Apart from very enthusiastic learners, there are often lots of children and sometimes
even animals to contend with, so your crowd control needs to be very good. Put some garden rules
in place before you start such as – respect the teacher by not talking while he is talking; everyone
to remain outside the garden perimeter unless asked to come to fulfil a task; tasks should be
completed 1 row at a time and then hand the task on to another learner; appropriate questions will



                                                  97
be answered after each step. Ask a helper to assist you with this. Sometimes if a crowd is talkative,
get them to sing a song while the tasks are getting done to get everyone in tune with what is
happening.


Time Management
It is also important to do good time management at your Well Watered Garden so that it can be
comfortably completed in 2.5-3 hours maximum. In order to do this as a trainer you need to drive
the step-by-step procedure. The longest exercise is holing out and furrows, so use a stopwatch
and see who is faster between the different men or women or youth groups. Be wary of particular
learners who are there simply to side track you with irrelevant questions, and suggest that they
come to you with their questions after the Well Watered Garden is complete.


Integration of the Biblical, Management and Technology Keys
Remember to integrate Biblical, Management and Technology keys throughout the practical, so the
learners can see how all the spheres fit together in reality on the farm or in their garden. For
example, do the Biblical Key 6 “Stake your Claim” at the garden and then pray over the garden
before starting any physical work. There are lots of opportunities to integrate all of the three
spheres throughout this practical session.
Well Watered Gardens are great fun, so enjoy yourself and be creative by using drama and short
stories to illustrate your points. At the same time, the Well Watered Garden must be taken very
seriously and learners must be challenged to do what they have heard and seen, and not just be
hearers only. Ask your prayer supporters to cover you in prayer throughout your trainings so that
the fullness of Jesus’ promised abundant life can be revealed in the community you are serving.
To reiterate, we are not trying to teach people to plant Well Watered Gardens. A Well Watered
Garden cannot make a family sustainably profitable, nor can it feed a family.
Instead we are using a Well Watered Garden in order to teach people how to implement Farming
God's Way on their own farm fields. Well Watered Gardens are simply small scale demonstrations
of what should be done at field scale.




                                                 98
12. 20 Reasons Why We Do the How

There are literally hundreds of testimonies of farmers like Dickson who have had their lives and
livelihoods transformed by the Lord’s grace and favour, with their yields improving from 3
bags/acre to 69 bags/acre. This is so encouraging and speaks of a real transformation from the
bottom up, without any handouts or handups or cash loans – just the impartation of knowledge,
which has been put into action.
By following the secrets that our heavenly Father has revealed to us in His incredible creation as
closely as possible, within a manageable system, we will obviously reap the rewards of His ways.
The 6 technology keys are:
1) No Ploughing
2) 100% cover with God’s blanket that is 2.5cm thick;
3) practising biodiversity;
4) God has created plants to feed from the top;
5) each plant has a unique canopy cover which allows us to determine plant population densities;
6) permanence.

Understanding exactly why God’s ways are so effective is what this chapter is all about and is
called 20 reasons why we do the how.

A) General Benefits

1) Minimal Runoff and 2) Minimal Erosion
  Experiment 1: Two runoff trays




Let’s take a look at an experiment to show the effects of God’s Blanket on runoff rates and erosion.
Each 20 litre container has the same amount of soil with the difference being that the conventional


                                                  99
farming trial has no cover and the Farming God's Way trial has God’s Blanket. By pouring just 2
litres of water over each container the results are dramatic.
You can clearly see how the runoff on a conventionally ploughed field is very high and as the water
gathers momentum down the slope it erodes the soil surface firstly and then forms rills and
ultimately even causes gullies to develop.
In the Farming God's Way trial, even though we used the same “ploughed soil” but covered it with
God’s Blanket, the runoff is very low and as a result, the soil erosion is minimal.
Rainfall trials done at Cedara in South Africa, applying 68mm on a 4% slope, showed that with
conventional ploughing, after 1 hour of rainfall, 90% of the applied rainfall was lost in runoff, along
with 28.5 tons of soil was lost in erosion per hectare.
By comparison, the no ploughing trial with just 80% mulch surface cover, lost only 6% of the rainfall
in runoff and a mere 1 ton of soil was lost per hectare. [12] These are incredible facts and on their
own, they should be enough evidence to convince farmers to turn to the way God has shown us.
What is the most limiting factor to yield in Africa? Rainfall, and yet we are rejecting the very gift that
God has given us, losing 90% of the rainfall we receive.
What is our most precious resource to hand down through future agriculture generations? Our soil,
and yet we are rejecting God’s gift to us. Conservative soil scientists suggest that the African
average annual soil loss is around 55 tons per hectare per annum, but it has been proven to be as
high as 250 tons per hectare in some regions under conventional tillage. This is unfaithfulness with
the talents God has entrusted to us.

3) Improved Infiltration
Experiment 2: Bread crust infiltration
Why do we lose so much water through runoff when we plough? Rain drops fall at very high speed
like little hammers, hitting the surface of the bare ground. Their impact causes a layer of
compaction called a crust, just like that on a loaf of bread.
Let’s take a look at an example of this effect with a loaf of bread.
When pouring just 250ml of water on the crusted loaf, we collect 200ml of runoff water.
That is because the crust seals the bread and stops the water from penetrating deeper. This is
exactly what happens when we plough; the first drops of rain create a crust and this allows only
10% of the rainfall to go into the soil and is very wasteful.

                             Raindrop impact on bare soil surface




                                                                            Splash droplets
                                                                           and soil particles



                                                                          Raindrop hammer action
                                                                            causing compaction
                                                                              and crust effect


                                                                          Soil airspaces important
                                                                          for moisture penetration




                                                  100
When we pour 250ml of water on the uncrusted surface…. we collect just 50ml of runoff water i.e.
4 times as much water penetrates the uncrusted bread. God’s Blanket protects the soil from the
hammer action of the raindrop by absorbing the impact like a shock absorber. When the raindrop’s
kinetic energy is dispersed, the water gradually moves down to the soil surface, through the
blanket and because there is no crust, it is rapidly absorbed. Once the water reaches the
unploughed soil it rapidly infiltrates down the many insect, earthworm and root channels, just like a
sponge. In this way we receive the gift of God and very little moisture is wasted.
The reasons why the Cedara experiment showed a 94% infiltration rate is because of the
absorption of kinetic energy of the raindrops by God’s Blanket and the sponge-like soil conditioning
by the living organisms and from not ploughing.

4) Evaporative Loss
Experiment 3: 2 Evaporation trays
The 10% of the moisture that infiltrates ploughed soil gets exposed to very high surface
temperatures as it is exposed to the sun, causing significant moisture losses through evaporation.
Naked soil surface temperatures over 65ºC have been recorded and you can imagine how much
evaporation takes place under these extremes of heat.
Leave the two runoff trays used in experiment 1 in the sun for 5 hours or more and then compare
the evaporative loss. The soil in the conventional tray dries out quickly and becomes hard like a
clay brick and cracks. The soil in the Farming God's Way tray, with 100 percent God’s blanket
cover 2.5cm thick, shades the soil surface keeping it cool and moist. The blanket significantly
reduces evaporation from the soil and is a major benefit to Farming God's Way success stories,
even under drought conditions.

5) Cooler Soil Temperature is Better for Seedling Growth
Cooler soil temperatures can be a negative initially as this causes slower germination and early
growth when compared to ploughed and naked lands, but remember that the cropping cycle is 5
months long, not 2 weeks, so be committed to the long term impacts. Once the seedlings are
established and have pushed through the blanket, they flourish and overtake neighbouring fields
within the first 3 weeks after emergence, as they have cooler temperatures for root establishment.

6) Don’t Waste the First Rain
Most farmers wait until the first rains before ploughing. After their first ploughing, they then have to
wait again, as the soil dries out so rapidly because of the poor infiltration and high evaporative
losses. After receiving enough rain for planting, they have to wait again for at least 2-4 days before
they can get their workers or tractors into the fields, otherwise it just becomes a muddy swamp.
This is an expensive and wasteful exercise. When we turn to Farming God's Way, we are able to
prepare our fields for planting by digging our holes well before the first rains so we won’t waste any
of God’s precious gift. After the rains, we are able to get into the fields almost immediately, with no
negative effects, because the soil is undisturbed and holds its structure. So we save our first rain
and are able to get in to plant much sooner after sufficient rain has fallen.

7) Improved Weed Control
Weeds are a major cause of crop losses as they compete with the crop for moisture, light, nutrients
and space all the time. Ploughing actually stimulates weed germination as it exposes weed seed to
light and scratches the weed seed coat.
We have seen this season how much a 100% cover of God’s Blanket has suppressed weed
growth in our Farming God's Way stands. The volunteers that kept on building up God’s Blanket on
their stands, only did 2 pre-canopy weedings and one post harvest weeding. The weedings were
light and very quick.




                                                  101
One of the volunteers was very busy over the season so he couldn’t give the management needed
to his crop and didn’t put on enough blanket. As a result, the weeds in his field went crazy and he
really struggled to keep them in check. His crop yields suffered drastically because of the loss of
moisture and nutrients stolen by the weeds. He had 3 weedings to do before canopy closure, all of
which took more than double the time and were hard work.
Therefore, no ploughing and 100% surface cover that is 2.5cm thick helps to significantly reduce
weed growth.

B) Soil Improvement Benefits
For the first 2-3 years the benefits, especially of moisture conservation, are the major reasons for
yield and economic improvements, but thereafter there are significant benefits to Farming God's
Way determined by the changes occurring as the soil regains its original crumbly structure.

8) Improved Water Holding Capacity
Experiment 4: 2 sponges
This crumbly macro-structure greatly improves the soil’s sponge effect or water holding capacity.
In this experiment take 2 sponges, exactly the same size, and chop one of them up finely to
represent a ploughed soil and the other one keep as is to represent an unploughed soil.
Dipping the ploughed soil sponge into the water shows that it collects just 200ml.
Whereas dipping the unploughed sponge into the water shows that it collects 350ml.
This simply shows that soil with an unploughed sponge-like structure is far better at holding
moisture, which in turn will result in better yields.

Experiment 5: 2 sponges
The miraculous effects of God’s Blanket in preventing erosion have already been explained, but
another factor is how ploughed soil is far more susceptible to erosion than unploughed soil.
Recently I had a laugh with a Ugandan man who said to me “now I understand why we have been
growing rocks in our lands, because our soil has been washing away each year”. The same
sponges can show us this fact very easily. Use a flexible container, like a coke bottle, with a hole in
the lid and squeeze 10 seconds of water onto the “ploughed sponge”. The result is a mess of little
bits of sponge everywhere!!! Whereas a 10 second blast on the “unploughed sponge” shows no
disturbance at all.

9) Improved Fertility
When we burn God’s Blanket we lose most of the precious nutrients found in crop residues into the
atmosphere. When we plough them in, we also lose out because the bacteria break down the plant
material and release the nutrients, but the organic matter gets oxidized so fast that it has no long
term benefit to soil fertility. Leading soil scientists say that in the early years of ploughing you halve
your soil organic carbon levels each year you plough. Therefore it takes years to rebuild an
impoverished soil.
Whereas when we start to accumulate God’s Blanket, it gradually decomposes over the years
through insect and other microbial activity. This is an important part of releasing the nutrients back
into the soil again. God in His wisdom has placed the blanket on top of the ground, where it
gradually breaks down into dark coloured humus and then into soil organic matter. Soil organic
matter is called black gold as it is such a good indicator of soil fertility. Black gold can hold a lot of
moisture as well as increase the cation exchange capacity (CEC) of the soil considerably so that
the soil can retain nutrients and keep them in the root zones, instead of them being lost through
leaching.




                                                   102
10) Nitrogen Fixing through Rotations with Legumes
Soil fertility is also improved by rotating grain crops with legumes such as beans, soyabeans,
groundnuts and other crops, which fix nitrogen. This nitrogen will be available for the following
year’s crop. The yield improvements after rotations are quite substantial and are a definite part of
God’s plan in reducing input requirements, as the soil’s fertility improves yearly with good
management. It is also advisable to include legumes in your cover crops, to ensure you get these
benefits in the off season as well.

11) Reduced Compaction

Experiment 6: 2 clods in 2 glasses
Besides the negative effect of crusting as already explained, ploughed exposed soil is also
susceptible to slumping, which is the collapse of the whole profile over time, as the structure and
air spaces get pulverized, making the soil much more compacted. By practicing no ploughing it is
interesting to hear of Farming God's Way farmers joyfully commenting that when they walk on their
fields the soil seems spongy to them!!!
Collect a clod of soil from a ploughed field and another from an unploughed field, preferably under
God’s Blanket. Take note that the ploughed clod is hard as a rock and the unploughed is soft and
crumbly. Place the soil clod from the ploughed land in the glass of water and you will see an
immediate collapse of the outer surface and a gradual collapse over time of the clod altogether.
The clod from the unploughed field remains very stable in the water and does not collapse as it is
being held together by roots, organic matter, insect glues and fungal strands. This is what we call a
water stable aggregate and it clearly demonstrates how much more stable and resistant to erosion
Farming God's Way soil is.
Ploughing also causes plough pans, which are hard soil compaction layers, which form at 20-30cm
deep and are caused by regular ploughing and heavy equipment shear lines. They can be so hard
that no roots can grow through them, limiting even 10m deep beautiful soil to a mere 30cm deep
over the years and they must be broken in order to get good yields. They can be broken using a
ripper or a pick line, where the pan just needs to be shattered, not uplifted.
Farming God's Way soil actually increases its volume over the years and has many old root
channels and insect burrows which allows for next year’s crops roots to move quickly down
through the profile, unrestricted.

12) Improved Aeration

Experiment 7: Flick 2 glasses with clods in to show bubbles
The unploughed clod will release many more bubbles when it is flicked. The sponge effect, from
not ploughing, through insect tunnels and decomposing root channels plays an important role in
keeping air locked in the channels, which will allow the roots to breathe even in waterlogged
conditions. Farmers believe that ploughing is essential for soil aeration and while this is true shortly
after ploughing, the soil soon collapses and ends up in a far less aerated state than it was at first.

13) Soil Microbiology Improvement
Soil is far more than just dust and one of the most recent advancements in soil science has been
gaining an understanding of the benefits of God’s creatures to the soil growing medium.
There are two basic types of soil microbes, namely aerobic which are those that need oxygen to
breathe, and anaerobic which are those that breathe carbon dioxide. Man in his wisdom inverts the
soil through ploughing and buries the aerobic organisms in the anaerobic environment and lifts the
anaerobic organisms into the aerobic environment. Yes, you guessed it – they all die as a result,
leaving the soil lifeless. God created each organism for a purpose and man has upset that balance.
A soil filled with life is a healthy soil.
We must be faithful to care for God’s garden and all the creatures in it, managing them in such a
way that they will allow our soils to be fertile and produce good yields. By not burning the blanket


                                                  103
we create an ideal cool and moist environment, for an abundance of organic life. By not ploughing
we allow these organisms to serve us, where insects and worms become our little ploughs,
keeping our soils full of channels for good infiltration of water and allowing for good aeration.
Others fertilize for us by depositing the soil organic matter or black gold right into the root zone.
Others break down the organic matter to make the nutrients more available for plant uptake. They
are all a part of the Lord’s sovereign plan for us to live in His promised abundant life.

C) Economic Benefits

The economic benefits of Farming God's Way are just as significant and, on their own merit,
should suffice to cause a change from conventional practices like ploughing and burning.

14) Pest and Disease Control
Many farmers are fearful of pests and disease infestation when shifting to Farming God's Way,
because of all the microbial life, but quite the opposite has been proven to take place. Plants under
moisture or nutrient stress actually give off wavelengths of light like an attractant, which encourage
infestations of pests and diseases. Our bodies are just the same. We are more susceptible to
getting sick when we are under stress. So the first line of resistance against pests is a healthy plant
that is not under stress. Secondly, the best long term remedy against pests is actually other insect
predators and therefore a healthy balanced soil ecosystem is the safest environment to prevent an
infestation in the first place. For example, root nematodes are problematical in the soil, but less
than 30 of the 100 types eat roots. Ploughing actually encourages the dominance of problematic
root nematodes, whereas no ploughing controls root nematodes by making room for their many
natural predators.
Monocropping, or growing the same crops year after year, also encourages major pest and
disease build up. Whereas the practise of crop rotations, breaks pest and disease lifecycles and
significantly reduces crop losses through their infestations.

15) Reduced Field Preparation Cost and Time
In mechanized, commercial practise, farmers deep plough, inverting the soil. Thereafter they
harrow at least twice and sometimes even 3 times, in order to break down the heavy clods they
have created. All this is done before planting. It has been proven that commercial farmers spend
three times as much on fuel, oil, tines, maintenance and repairs of machinery, compared to a no
ploughing technique. Three times!!! Imagine what that does to affect profitability.
Small scale farmers are spending up to 10 weeks ploughing and preparing their fields, compared
to just a few weeks to complete the holes and put the inputs in, ready for planting with Farming
God's Way. Our trials have revealed that Farming God's Way fields account for 30% of the cost in
land preparation, meaning you save 70% of the costs as compared to conventional farming land
preparation. Time and labour costs in land preparation are resources not to be wasted.

16) Reduced Fertiliser Losses
Every year millions of bags of fertiliser are lost into the catchments through runoff losses, leaching
and erosion. These losses account for billions of dollars wasted yearly. In the catchments, this
wasted fertiliser causes environmental disasters in dams and rivers. Fertiliser is very expensive so
we should use it wisely, place it accurately and preserve it in the root zone. Farming God’s way
accomplishes all of these objectives most efficiently.

17) Improved Drought Tolerance and Risk Spread
A soil’s drought tolerance has been proven to depend on 3 main factors:
      the percentage cover of God’s Blanket
      good aggregation or a crumbly soil structure
      the amount of organic matter found in the soil



                                                 104
Remember Dickson’s testimony of how they had no rain from December to harvest time, and yet
he still harvested the equivalent of 30 bags of maize, compared to his neighbour’s 1,5 bags from
the same size land area. One year of crop failure is a significant economic loss and may lead to a
family dying of starvation, so take the necessary precautions as droughts and infrequent rains will
come.
Having your eggs in more than one basket is also regarded as good economic sense. The practise
of the 1/3rd rotation also helps to spread the risk of a total crop failure in a disaster such as drought
or regional pest outbreak.

18) Reduced Irrigation Costs
The number of times that a crop gets irrigated can be drastically reduced due to the moisture
conservation from no ploughing and having a high percentage of God’s Blanket. A 100% cover
would save four waterings as compared to no cover at all. Irrigation is very expensive and
therefore four waterings less could be the difference between a profit and a loss.

19) Improved Crop Efficiency
The most important factor to consider is the crop itself. Crops grown in burnt and ploughed fields
struggle to find moisture and nutrients and are regularly under stress. The stress causes them to
grow inefficiently by sending roots deep into the profile and they spend too much energy on the
below ground portion of their growth and not enough on the above ground portion. We don’t
harvest below the ground but above!!!
Farming God’s Way provides a plant with the ideal environment to flourish by limiting its stresses,
and so the plant grows much more efficiently and the result is greatly improved yields. The roots
grow right under the blanket accessing the high fertility of the decomposing blanket and enjoying
the readily available moisture in the upper levels of the profile.

20) Yield Improvements
Compare Dickson’s increase to his father’s average yield of 3 bags. In his first year he got 5;
second year 45; third year 54 and fourth year 69 bags. Joseph went from 7 to 70 bags in the first
year and George had a 9 times yield improvement. These yield improvements speak for
themselves.

                                                106
   13. Experiments and Benefits (Trainer Content)

It is very important that as a trainer you have the knowledge base to be able to communicate all of
the benefits of Farming God's Way over Conventional practices. However, time is often limited at
training events and this makes it quite difficult to teach all of the 20 Reasons why we do the how in
the allocated time, unless you are using the video materials. Therefore, it is advisable for you to
use the 5 main experiments which are all in the video series, as a guide to be able to cover most of
the primary benefits as you progress through them. Remember to keep on threading the 3 spheres
of influence – Biblical, Management and Technology - into all the experiments so that learners
don’t get locked into the technology alone.

        5 Main Experiments:
        1) Runoff Tray Experiment
        2) Bread Experiment
        3) Sponge Experiment
        4) Clod Experiment
        5) Evaporation Experiment


1) Runoff Tray Experiment – Runoff and Erosion
This experiment is a comparison of runoff from naked soil versus
runoff with God’s blanket. The main target points are the
comparative loss of water, soil and nutrients.

Experiment Kit:
i) Two Runoff trays - 20 litre bottle cut in half with holes drilled in the bottom.
ii) Two clear 2 litre bottles for collecting runoff.
Iii) Watering can made from a 2 litre bottle with small holes drilled into base. This helps to direct the
flow of water directly into the area of the tray you target.
iv) 10 litre bucket of water – this will be used in all the experiments.
Experimental Procedure:
Fill each tray with dry soil to 5mm below the spout and then place the trays on a 15 degree slope.
Apply a 2.5cm thick layer of God’s blanket, making sure to place it across the slope. Ask an
assistant to catch the water using a 2 litre bottle on each side. Immerse the 2 litre watering
can/bottle into the 10 litre bucket of water, lift it as high as you can and pour the water onto the
naked soil tray first. Repeat the experiment with 2 litres of water poured onto the tray with 2.5cm of
God’s blanket on top of it. Hold both runoff collection bottles up and discuss the differences. Don’t
throw out the soil from the two trays as this will be used in experiment 5, nor the erosion and runoff
bottles, as they will be used to reinforce Farming God's Way principles such as “caring for the
garden” and “sustainable profitability” throughout the training.


2) Bread Experiment - Crusting Effect on Runoff
This experiment is a comparison of runoff off the crust of a half loaf of bread versus the runoff off a
porous/uncrusted half loaf of bread. The main target points are the hammer effect of raindrop
impact, which causes crusting, the 1st layer of compaction on naked soil, versus the blanket’s
ability to act like a “shock absorber” absorbing all of the kinetic energy of the raindrops which
prevents crusting and leaves soil in a porous and permeable state which reduces runoff and
improves infiltration.




                                                   107
Experiment Kit:
i) Loaf of bread cut in 2 halves
ii) One wide container to catch runoff off bread
iii) Two Clear 500ml bottles for collecting runoff
Experimental Procedure:
Hold the half loaf well above the open tray with the crusted end on the topside. Steadily pour 250ml
of water onto the crust, collecting the runoff in the wide container below, then pour the water into a
500ml bottle. Repeat the procedure with the other half loaf, however this time hold the cut porous
side of the loaf facing upwards and then pour the 250ml of water, then decant any runoff water
from the wide container into the other 500ml bottle. There should be between 3-4 times more water
from the crusted bread side. Hold both bottles up for discussion about the effect of crusting on
runoff and infiltration.

3) Sponge Experiment - Water Holding Capacity
This experiment is a comparison of the water holding capacity of a broken up sponge versus a
sponge that is still intact. The main target points are ploughing breaks up soil structure into powder,
reducing the soil’s porosity, aeration and water holding capacity.
Experiment Kit
i) Cut or break up one 10cm × 15cm sponge into very small pieces, smaller than 2mm, and put
them into a short stocking and tie the end off. This will simulate a ploughed soil.
ii) One 10cm × 15cm standard sponge
iii) One wide container to catch squeezed water from sponges
iv) Two Clear 500ml bottles

Experimental Procedure
Immerse the stocking/ploughed sponge into the 10 litre bucket and squeeze it until all the air
bubbles stop coming to the top. Ask an observer to watch the number of air bubbles coming out.
Lift the sponge up and wait for the water to stop running out, then move the sponge over the wide
container and squeeze all the water out. Pour the water into a 500ml bottle.
Take the unploughed standard sponge and immerse it in the water and again squeeze it until all
the air bubbles are gone. Ask the observer to comment on the increased aeration of the
unploughed sponge. Lift the sponge up, being careful to balance it in your hand, so that as much
water is retained as possible. Once the excess has drained into the 10L bucket move it over to the
wide container and squeeze out all the water, then pour this into your 500ml bottle. Depending on
the sponge porosity you should easily get 2-3 times more water. You may have to even fill more
than 1 bottle - count the number of 500ml bottles filled. Hold up the bottles and discuss the effect of
ploughing on porosity, aeration and water holding capacity.

4) Clod Experiment - Water Stable Aggregates
This experiment is a comparison of the aggregate stability of two different clods of soil when placed
into water; the one clod from a ploughed field versus a clod from a forest floor or a long standing
Farming God's Way field.

Experiment Kit
i) Two clear 2 litre bottles for clod experiment (can be cut short)
ii) Two ploughed field clods - fist sized
iii) Two pristine soil clods - fist sized




                                                     108
Experimental Procedure
Fill both bottles with water to ¾ level. Take one of the ploughed field clods and demonstrate how
lightly coloured, hard and non-porous it is, and how it crumbles into powder when crushed. Take
the other ploughed field clod, and slowly immerse it into the bottle of water and hold it for as long
as you can, to show how it collapses in water, ending up in a big cloud of mud at the bottom with
little to no bubbles.
Next take the clod from the forest and demonstrate how dark, soft and porous it is and how it has a
crumbly texture when crushed. Take the other forest clod and hold it in the water and release it.
Some clods have been known to float for some time and they will give off many bubbles for a long
time when the bottle is flicked. This clod will in most cases not break down during 3 day trainings.
Hold both bottles up and talk through colour, organic matter, aeration, texture and water stable
aggregates. Talk through the 2nd form of compaction “slumping” as a result of conventionally
ploughed soils being water unstable as well as using the visible fine suspended clay particles to
explain the 3rd form of compaction “plough pans”. Don’t throw away the clod bottles as they will be
used to reinforce principles of limiting slumping and soil improvements throughout the training.


5) Evaporation Experiment – Evaporation from Trays
This experiment follows on from the runoff trials in experiment 1. Experiment no.5 is usually
mentioned at the end of the experiment session in a question form of “what do you expect to
happen?” However, the experiment will only show its benefits after a day or two. Leave the trays
out in the sun every day and then compare the two trays to observe the relative moisture content
and crusting, from the ploughed and naked soil versus the tray with 2.5cm of God’s Blanket on top
of it. During the session on minimal wastage, use the experimental props to reinforce the
conservation of water, soil and nutrients.


Summary
Summarise all of the experiments by running through each one in short at the end.


The main points to reiterate are how Farming God's Way helps to conserve water, soil & nutrients:

1) water – through reduced runoff, deep penetration and improved water holding capacity;

2) soil – reduced erosion, improved structure, organic matter and fertility;

3) nutrients – through reduced losses with runoff, leaching and organic matter.


Also reiterate how Farming God's Way affects the 3 forms of compaction:
1) crusting;

2) slumping;

3) plough pans.


Reinforce the links of Biblical keys, management and technology, all the while remembering to
mention the target of sustainable profitability, which is still the biggest motivator for change to occur
amongst farmers.


For a full checklist of what you need for the experiments, see the appendix.




                                                  109
14. Biodiversity Through Alternative Crops

A part of God’s revelation through His creation, is the biodiversity of species that exists under
natural conditions. Although we cannot match this biodiversity, we encourage farmers to adopt this
principle of plant biodiversity by practising Rotations, Rotations within Rotations, Relay Cropping
and Cover Cropping.

   14.1.       Crop Rotations
Traditionally farmers plant the same crops year after year, which is called monocropping. In
contrast to this, crop rotation is a practise of regularly planting different crops in the same area in
alternating seasons. Rotations are essential for maintaining healthy soils, healthy crops, and
controlling pests and diseases, to name just a few benefits, and yet this simple practise is one of
the hardest concepts for farmers to grasp.
In field scale agronomy, we advocate a very simple rotation practise of dividing every cropland into
three equal portions, with a rotation every 3rd year.

                                    Year 1              Year 2           Year 3             Year 4
           Portion 1                 Maize              Beans             Maize             Maize
           Portion 2                 Maize              Maize             Beans             Maize
           Portion 3                Beans               Maize             Maize             Beans


This common maize and beans example clearly shows a 3 part rotation plan. In the 1st year, the 1st
two portions will be planted to the staple crop, and the 3rd portion planted to the rotational crop,
preferably a legume. In the 2nd year, the rotational crop will move to portion 1 and the 2nd and 3rd
portions will be planted to the staple crop. In the 3rd year, the rotational crop will be planted in
portion 2 and the staple crop will be in the 1st and 3rd portions. This means that over a three year
period, each field portion will have two years of a staple crop and one year of the rotational crop.
See the Field Scale Layout diagram in the Section – “Small Scale Implementation”.
The Vegetable rotation plan is discussed in the “Farming God's Way Vegetable Guide” with Fruit,
Leaf and Root portion rotations in a six monthly seasonal cycle.
Rotations have great advantages over monocropping systems, including:
   1. providing for protein and vitamins in family diets
   2. breaking disease and pest cycles
   3. fixing of nitrogen by legumes, which results in cost savings and yield improvements for the
      following season’s crops
   4. improving soil structure
   5. spreading of financial risk
Rotational crops are many and the variations thereof are innumerable, however here are some
general guidelines for planning your rotations:
The rotation should have a change from a monocotyledonous to a dicotyledonous species.
Rotational crops should preferably include a legume such as beans, soyabeans, pigeon peas,
cowpeas, sugar beans or groundnuts, but can include sunflower, sweet potato or vegetables.
The 3rd rotation portion can also be subdivided further to include a variety of vegetables for a
family’s nutritional supplement.
There is no “one size fits all plan” for rotations, so select the rotational crop based on your climate,
soil and objectives, then prepare and manage the rotation crop according to its specifications by
adjusting inputs, spacing, seeding rates, depth of planting, thinning and topdressing.


                                                  110
If you don’t practise rotations, you are going to encounter problems typical of a monocropping
system, such as an increase in pest and disease incidence, increased problematic weeds and a
decline in soil fertility. Rotations play a significant role in ensuring we reach the “sustainable
profitability” objective of Farming God's Way management.

   14.2.       Rotations within Rotations
Another way we ensure biodiversity is by planting different “short growing season crops” after
harvesting other “short growing season crops”, even within the same rotation time period. This is
especially important when looking at crops with growing cycles less than 4 months in duration. This
is to ensure that pests that are common to those crops don’t get the opportunity to increase
exponentially with host plants planted twice over in the same season.
An example of this would be to plant cowpeas directly after a crop of early variety groundnuts in a
single rainfall period.
However, Rotations within Rotations are more commonly done with short season vegetable crops.
The Farming God's Way Vegetable rotation plan is based on Fruit, Leaf and Root portion rotations
in a 6 monthly seasonal cycle. In a single vegetable 6 month rotation period we can easily get 3
root crop plantings. An example of rotations within rotations with root vegetables would be turnips
after beetroot after spring onions. See the “Farming God's Way Vegetable Guide” for more
information.

   14.3.       Relay Cropping
Mixed cropping is a common African practice, especially with farmers growing beans or pumpkin
between maize rows. However, neither of these crops can ever reach their yield potentials with this
system because of the negative effects of shading and competition for nutrients. As a result, we
discourage mixed cropping for manageability and crop performance reasons.
As an alternative to mixed cropping, we encourage plant biodiversity through relay cropping,
provided the climate or residual moisture allows for this. When running a relay race you pass the
baton from one exhausted runner to the next runner. In the same way, relay cropping is planting
the second crop when the first crop is at physiological maturity and dying back. Relay crops can be
planted in planting stations, in furrows or broadcast under the dying 1st crop canopy.
“Relay Cropping” is different to “Rotations within Rotations” as “Relay Crops” are planted before
harvesting the first crop, whereas with “Rotations within Rotations Crops” are planted after
harvesting the first crop. Take care to get your “On Time Management” done with relay cropping so
as not to plant the relay crop too early, as you will cause your first crop yield to suffer when the
grain fill stage is happening. Most relay crops are not planted with any inputs as they are good
scavengers, which will be able to find the leftover nutrients from the dying crop they are taking the
place of, whilst still producing a good harvest. “Relay Crops” are not true “Cover Crops” when they
are planted for the purpose of growing food or for animal grazing as there is a net extraction of
nutrients from the system, but they still offer many benefits.




                                                111
                              Pigeon Pea relay crop at maize harvest time

An example of Relay Cropping in Southern Africa is to plant pigeon peas when the maize leaves
just start to die back, usually at the end of February or March. By the end of April or early May, the
maize is ready for harvest, after which post-harvest stalk lodging will get done, by knocking down
the stalks between the pigeon peas which will be approximately 30cm tall. In September the Relay
Crop of pigeon peas will be ready for harvest. The benefits of this system include being able to
harvest two crops in a climate with only one rainy season; pigeon peas are a legume and fix
nitrogen for the following crop; increase in God’s Blanket cover; good weed control through shade
effect; as well as allowing farmers the right to protect their blanket from burning by rat hunters and
grazing by animals. Dickson Shuwali harvests an average of 750kg from his pigeon peas, which is
incredible in their dry, non rainy, winter season for a second crop.
Plant pigeon peas as a relay crop without inputs, in alternate planting stations 120cm apart and in
75cm rows when the maize is standing at full height and starting to die off. Simply dig halfway
between two maize planting stations to just 3cm deep, plant 3 seeds and cover level. Skip a 60cm
space and go to the next gap to make up the 120cm interval. Repeat this on every 75cm row line.
After emergence, thin down to just 2 seedlings per planting station. Further details for Pigeon Peas
are in the Alternative Crops section.
                                                Cowpeas are another very good option for a relay
                                                crop, as they have a short growing season, are
                                                drought tolerant and are very good at scavenging
                                                nutrients.
                                                Upright or bush type Cowpea varieties can be
                                                planted without inputs, by digging a shallow 3cm
                                                furrow, 15cm on the upslope side of each maize
                                                row, then planting 10cm apart and covering level. In
                                                this way when post harvest stalk lodging is done at
                                                30 degrees, the stalks will not interfere with the
                                                relay cowpea crop. Alternatively, upright cowpeas
                                                can be broadcast at maize die back, with a last light
                                                weeding conducted, which will allow for good seed
                                                to soil contact.
Vining Cowpea varieties can be planted at a much lower population density in planting stations
60cm apart and in 75cm rows. Dig 3cm deep planting stations between the standing maize
planting stations, plant 2 seeds and cover level. They will soon close the canopy and shade out
any weeds. See the “Alternative Crops” section for more details.



                                                 112
   14.4.       Cover Crops
Cover crops are defined as crops grown for the protection and enrichment of the soil, rather than
for the purpose of being harvested. Cover crops are an amazing part of God’s all sufficiency, and
given the right climates and management, can easily be integrated into cropping cycles. Cover
crops have been used commercially for many years to build up soil organic matter and fertility,
increase mulch cover percentages, fix nitrogen, reduce fertilization costs, control weeds and
prevent erosion. Cover crops can also be grown to help condition new lands, or to help restore old
depleted lands for future seasons.
Crops that are grown for human or animal consumption are not true cover crops, as there is a net
extraction of nutrients from the soil. Cover crops are intentionally grown as an investment into the
soil and killed by slashing or rolling just before physiological maturity. Cover crops can be sown
after harvesting the main crop or using the relay technique. They can be sown in planting stations,
furrows or by broadcasting the seeds.
Cover Crops can be killed by cutting them down or by rolling at physiological maturity, but most
small scale farmers will simply use slashers or hoes to cut them down as close to the ground as
possible. The cut cover crop then becomes the blanket material you need for your main season’s
crop, so you are effectively growing your own blanket.
Being “On Time” to kill Cover Crops is very important, and should be done early enough to prevent
its seed from maturing and late enough for as much biomass to be produced as possible. Killing
small grains too early will result in vegetative regrowth from their tillers, as they are not
physiologically mature and they also haven’t gone into the production of long lasting biomass.
Killing Cover Crops too late will result in its seeds fully maturing, drying, later germinating and
becoming a “weed” amongst the main season’s crop.
Cover Crops in Southern Africa are usually restricted to cold tolerant species, which usually
coincide with the dry season including cowpeas, vetch and oats. These are all productive and
economical examples of cover crops that are well suited to broadcasting under the canopy before
the last weeding at leaf die back.
Cover Crops are also well suited to the climates of Central and West Africa, where there are
extended rainfall seasons. In bimodal rainfall regions such as in Uganda, it is suggested to grow
cover crops in the short rain periods to replenish the soil, control weeds and provide blanket cover
for the main cropping season.
Some excellent leguminous Cover Crops include dolichos lablab, mucuna/velvet bean, cowpeas,
peas and vetch. Leguminous Cover Crops are excellent at fixing nitrogen and improving soil
fertility. However, they also breakdown very quickly because of their low Carbon to Nitrogen ratio
and therefore don’t make a long lasting blanket.
Examples of monocotyledon Cover Crops include oats, triticale, wheat, stooling rye and fodder
sorghum. The monocots produce a very good blanket cover because of their high carbon to
nitrogen ratio and are excellent at weed suppression, but not as good as the dicots at improving
soil fertility. So it is preferable to make up
mixtures of 2:1 or 3:1 monocots to dicots
ratio, to ensure the balance of benefits is
achieved.
At the model Farming God's Way farm in Port
Elizabeth no weeding has been done in the
Farming God's Way Comparative Gardens for
5 years, because of the excellent weed
suppression by the Cover Crop while it is
growing, as well as the thick blanket it
produces once it has been cut down.
                                                            Cover Crop of Black Oats and Vetch




                                                113
   14.5.          Alternative Crop Guidelines
A common misconception is that Farming God’s Way is teaching farmers about maize and beans.
In the garden in the east of Eden, every plant that was good for food was grown using God’s ways
from the very beginning, and there is no reason why all plants cannot be grown in the way He has
revealed to us through His creation. This section will give you some guidelines on how to grow
some of the alternative crop types using our preferred organic inputs of compost, manure or anthill
soil, or alternatively the options for inorganic input users. If your soils are very depleted, you
increase the 350ml organic input to 500ml for 2 to 3 years to invest in your crop production while
your soils are slowly improving. Take note when planting legumes, that most depleted soils have
no naturally occurring nitrogen fixing bacteria and therefore you must inoculate them with
Rhizobium bacteria and plant your legume seed within 6 hours, to have the optimal nitrogen fixing
effect.

                             Cassava can be grown effectively on flat ground. Cut the plants that are
